### II / 二

===Original===
Next day, in Cedarwood, a woman in mourning—in mourning fluffed and jetted and furbelowed in such pleasing fashion that it seemed flamboyantly to demand immediate consolation of all marriageable males—viewed me with a roving eye as I heaped daffodils on Stella’s grave. They had cost me a pretty penny, too, for this was in September. But then I must have daffodils, much as I loathe the wet, limp feel of them, because she would have chosen daffodils. … Well! I fancied this woman thought me sanctioned by both church and law in what I did—and viewed me in my supposedly recent bereavement and gauged my potentialities—viewed me, in short, with the glance of adventurous widowhood.
===Chinese===
第二天，在雪松墓园（Cedarwood），一位身着丧服的女人——那丧服缀满蓬松的绉纱、黑玉坠子和层层荷叶边，款式俏丽悦目，俨然招摇地要求一切适婚男性即刻上前慰藉——用一只溜来溜去的眼睛打量我，正当我把一捧捧黄水仙堆上斯特拉的坟头。这可是花了我好大一笔钱的，要知道这时节已是九月，水仙早不当令。可我偏就非要黄水仙不可，尽管我厌恶它们那湿渍渍、软塌塌的手感，因为换作是她，她必定挑黄水仙。……嗐！我料想这位太太只当我此举是得了教会与法律双重认可的——她把我看作一个想必新遭丧偶的男人，掂量着我的种种潜能——简而言之，她打量我的那一眼，是富于冒险精神的寡妇的一眼。

===Original===
My faith (I meditated) if she knew!—if I could but speak my thought to her!
===Chinese===
我的老天（我心里暗暗盘算），她若是知道才好呢！——我若是能把心里这番话说给她听才好呢！

===Original===
“Madam,”—let us imagine me, my hat raised, my voice grave—“the woman who lies here was a stranger to me. I did not know her. I knew that her eyes were blue, that her hair was sunlight, that her voice had pleasing modulations; but I did not know the woman. And she cared nothing for me. That is why my voice shakes as I tell you of it. And I have brought her daffodils, because of all flowers she loved them chiefly, and because there is no one else who remembers this. It is the flower of spring, and Stella—for that was her name, madam—died in the spring of the year, in the spring of her life; and Stella would have been just twenty-six today. Oh, and daffodils, madam, are all white and gold, even as that handful of dust beneath us was all white and gold when we buried it with a flourish of crepe and lamentation, some two years and five months ago. Yet the dust there was tender flesh at one time, and it clad a brave heart; but we thought of it—and I among the rest—as a plaything with which some lucky man might while away his leisure hours. I believe now that it was something more. I believe—ah, well, my credo is of little consequence. But whatever this woman may have been, I did not know her. And she cared nothing for me.”
===Chinese===
「夫人，」——且让我们设想一下我这副模样：举着帽子，嗓音庄重——「长眠在此的这位女人，于我是一个陌生人。我并不认识她。我知道她的眼睛是蓝的，她的头发是阳光的颜色，她的嗓音有着悦耳的抑扬顿挫；可我并不认识这个女人。而她呢，也全不把我放在心上。正因这一层，我对您说起这些的时候，声音才直发颤。我给她带来了黄水仙，因为万花之中她最爱这个，也因为除我之外，再没有旁人记得这一层。这是春天的花，而斯特拉——她名唤斯特拉，夫人——死在一年的春天，死在她一生的春天；到今天，斯特拉本该恰好二十六岁了。哦，还有，夫人，黄水仙通体只是白与金黄，恰如你我脚下这一抔尘土——两年零五个月前，我们伴着翻飞的黑纱与一片哭丧声将它下葬时，它也是通体的白与金黄。然而那一抔尘土，从前也曾是温软的血肉，包裹过一颗勇敢的心；可我们从前把它看作——我也是其中一分子——一件玩意儿，哪个交好运的男人都尽可以拿它来消磨闲散的时辰。如今我相信，它远不止于此。我相信——嗐，罢了，我信的是什么，无关宏旨。可无论这个女人从前究竟如何，我并不认识她。而她也全不把我放在心上。」

===Original===
I reflected I would like to do it. I could imagine the stare, the squawk, the rustling furbelows, as madam fled from this grave madman. She would probably have me arrested.
===Chinese===
我寻思着，真想当真这么干一场。那位太太从我这坟前的疯子跟前逃开去时的一瞪眼、一声尖叫、窸窸窣窣乱响的荷叶边，我都能想象得出。她说不定还会叫人把我抓起来。

===Original===
You see I had come to think differently of Stella. At times I remembered her childish vanity, her childish, morbid views, her childish gusts of petulance and anger and mirth; and I smiled—oh, very tenderly, yet I smiled.
===Chinese===
你瞧，我对斯特拉的想法，如今已与从前不同了。有时我记起她孩子气的虚荣，她孩子气的、病态的见地，她孩子气的一阵阵使性子、动怒与欢闹；我便微笑了——哦，无限温柔地微笑，然而我到底是微笑了。

===Original===
Then would awake the memory of Stella and myself in that ancient moonlight and of our first talk of death—two infants peering into infinity, somewhat afraid, and puzzled; of Stella making tea in the firelight, and prattling of her heart’s secrets, half-seriously, half in fun; and of Stella striving to lift a very worthless man to a higher level and succeeding—yes, for the time, succeeding; and of Stella dying with a light heart, elate with dreams of Peter Blagden’s future and of “a life that counted”; and of what she told me at the very last. And, irrationally perhaps, there would seem to be a sequence in it all, and I could not smile over it, not even tenderly.
===Chinese===
跟着便会惊醒另一串记忆：斯特拉和我在那片古老的月光里，头一回谈起死亡——两个婴孩朝无穷的深处窥望，有些害怕，又有些迷惑；斯特拉在炉火光里沏茶，半真半假地絮叨她心头的秘密；斯特拉竭力要把一个顶没出息的男人往高处提携，而且提成了——是的，至少那一时提成了；斯特拉怀着轻快的心走向死亡，满心飞扬着对彼得·布拉格登前程的梦想、对「一段有分量的生命」的梦想；还有她到了最后一刻对我说的话。而这一切，或许并无道理可言，却又仿佛丝丝相扣、自成一线，我便笑不出来了——连温柔地笑一笑，也做不到。

===Original===
And I would depicture her, a foiled and wistful little wraith, very lonely in eternity, and a bit regretful of the world she loved and of its blundering men, and unhappy—for she could never be entirely happy without Peter—and I feared, indignant. For Stella desired very heartily to be remembered—she was vain, you know—and they have all forgotten. Yes, I am sure that even as a wraith, Stella would be indignant, for she had a fine sense of her own merits.
===Chinese===
而我会把她描画成这样一幅图景：一个失意的、怅惘的小小亡魂，在永恒里形单影只，对她所爱的这个尘世、对尘世这班莽撞的男人，都有几分恋恋不舍，还有几分不快活——因为没有彼得，她永远快活不齐全——而且，我怕，还有几分愤愤不平。因为斯特拉巴心巴肝地盼望被人记着——她虚荣，你是知道的——可他们把她全忘了。是的，我确信，纵然做了亡魂，斯特拉也定要愤愤不平，因为她对自家的种种好处，向来别有一副精细的鉴赏力。

===Original===
“But I am just a little butterfly-woman,” she would say, sadly; then, with a quick smile, “Aren’t I?” And her eyes would be like stars—like big, blue stars—and afterward her teeth would glint of a sudden, and innumerable dimples would come into being, and I would know she was never meant to be taken seriously. …
===Chinese===
「可我只是个小蝴蝶女人呀，」她会怏怏地说；随即又飞快地一笑：「可不是么？」她的眼睛会像星星——像大大的、蓝蓝的星星——随后她的牙齿倏地一闪，数不清的笑涡霎时间一齐涌现，我便明白：她这个人，从来就不是造来叫人当真的。……

===Original===
But we must avoid all sickly sentiment.
===Chinese===
只是，一切病态感伤的情怀，我们都必须避而远之。

===Original===
You see the world had advanced since Stella died—twice around the sun, from solstice to solstice, from spring to winter and back again, travelling through I forget how many millions of miles; and there had been wars and scandals and a host of debutantes and any number of dinners; and, after all, the world is for the living.
===Chinese===
你瞧，自斯特拉死后，这世界又赶了一程路——绕着太阳足足转了两圈，从夏至到夏至，从春天到冬天、再从冬天回到春天，穿行过我不记得是多少百万英里的途程；这中间有过战争，有过丑闻，出过一大茬初入社交界的少女，摆过数不清的席面；再说到底，这世界终究是活人的。

===Original===
So we of Lichfield agreed unanimously that it was very sad, and spoke of her for a while, punctiliously, as “poor dear Stella”; and the next week Emily Van Orden ran away with Tom Whately; and a few days later Alicia Wade’s husband died, and we debated whether Teddy Anstrother would do the proper thing or sensibly marry Celia Reindan: and so, a little by a little, we forgot our poor, dear Stella in precisely the decorous graduations of regret with which our poor dear Stella would have forgotten any one of us.
===Chinese===
于是我们利奇菲尔德人众口一词，公认这事可悲得很，还循规蹈矩地管了她一阵子「可怜的亲爱的斯特拉」；过一个礼拜，埃米莉·范·奥登（Emily Van Orden）同汤姆·惠特利（Tom Whately）私奔了；再过几天，艾丽西娅·韦德的丈夫死了，我们又议论纷纷：特迪·安斯特瑟究竟会行一下得体的举动呢，还是索性放聪明些，娶了塞莉娅·赖因丹：就这样，一点一点地，我们把可怜的、亲爱的斯特拉忘了——那一套分寸井然、层层递减的哀悼，同我们那位可怜的、亲爱的斯特拉从前忘掉我们中间随便哪一位时所用的，恰恰是同一路数。

===Original===
Yes, even those who loved her most deeply have forgotten Stella. They remember only an imaginary being who was entirely perfect, and of whom they were not worthy. It is this fictitious woman who has usurped the real Stella’s place in the heart of the real Stella’s own mother, and whom even Lizzie d’Arlanges believes to have been once her sister, and over whom Peter Blagden is always ready to grow maudlin; and it is this immaculate woman—who never existed—that will be until the end of Avis’ matrimonial existence the standard by which Avis is measured and found wanting. And thus again the whirligig of time, by an odd turn, brings in his revenges.
===Chinese===
是的，连那些爱她爱得最深的人，也把斯特拉忘了。他们记得的，只是一个凭空想象出来、十全十美的人物，一个他们自惭不配的人物。正是这个虚构的女人，篡夺了真正的斯特拉在她亲生母亲心里的位置；连莉齐·达朗热都相信，这人从前就是她的妹妹；彼得·布拉格登一提起她，又随时预备涕泪涟涟地伤感一场；也正是这个纯洁无瑕、从来不曾存在过的女人，将直到艾维斯婚姻存续的尽头，都充当那杆衡量艾维斯的标尺，叫她一量便见亏欠。于是时间的旋轮，就这样又古怪地转出一记弯，把它一桩桩的报复带回来了（化用《第十二夜》弄人之歌「时间的旋轮就这样带来了它的报复」）。

===Original===
And I? Well, I was very fond of Stella. And the woman they speak of today, in that hushed, hateful, sanctimonious voice, I must confess I never knew. And of all persons I chiefly rage against that faultless angel, that “poor dear Stella,” who has pilfered even the paltry tribute of being remembered from the Stella that today is mine alone. For it is to this fictitious person that the people whom my Stella loved, as she did not love me, now bring their flowers; and it was to this person they erected their pompous monument—nay, more, it was for this atrocious woman they ordered the very coffin in which my Stella lay when I last saw her. And it is not fair.
===Chinese===
那么我呢？嗐，我是非常喜欢斯特拉的。可他们如今用那种压低的、可憎的、假道学的声气谈论的那个女人，恕我直言，我从来不曾认识。而在天下众生之中，我最切齿痛恨的，正是那位无可指摘的天使，那位「可怜的亲爱的斯特拉」——因为她连「被人记得」这一点点微薄的祭礼，也从如今只属于我一人的那个斯特拉身上偷走了。因为我那位斯特拉所爱的那些人——她可不曾这样爱过我——如今捧着鲜花献去的，正是这个凭空捏造的人物；他们树起那座浮华的纪念碑，为的是这个人物；岂止如此，他们定做那口棺材——我最后一眼见到我那位斯特拉时她躺着的那口——为的也正是这个穷凶极恶的女人。这实在不公平。

===Original===
And I? Well, I was very fond of Stella. It would be good to have her back—to have her back to jeer at me, to make me feel red and uncomfortable and ridiculous, to say rude things about my waist, and indeed to fluster me just by being there. Yes, it would be good. But, upon the whole, I am not sorry that Stella is gone.
===Chinese===
那么我呢？嗐，我是非常喜欢斯特拉的。她若能回来，倒也着实好——回来到我跟前讥诮我，叫我脸上发红、浑身不自在、自觉可笑，对我的腰身说些无礼的话，甚而至于，只消人往那儿一站，便够叫我心慌意乱。是的，那倒也好。可是，总的说来，斯特拉已经去了，我并不惋惜。

===Original===
For there is Peter Blagden to be considered. We can all agree today that Peter is a good fellow, that he is making the most of his Uncle Larry’s money, and that he is nobody’s enemy but his own; and we have smugly forgotten the time when we expected him to become a great lawyer. We do not expect that of Peter now; instead, we are content enough—particularly since Peter has so admirably dressed his part by taking to longish hair and gruffness and a cane—to point him out to strangers in Lichfield as “one of our wealthiest men,” and to elect him to all civic committees, and to discuss his semiannual sprees and his monetary relations with various women whom one does not “know.” And the present Mrs. Blagden, too, appears content enough.
===Chinese===
因为还有彼得·布拉格登这层要考虑进去。今天我们大家尽可以一致公认：彼得是个好人，他把他拉里叔叔的钱使唤得淋漓尽致，他谁的对头也不是——只除了他自个儿；我们还沾沾自喜地忘掉了当年巴望他成为大律师的那段时光。如今我们再不对彼得抱那种指望了；非但如此，我们还相当知足——尤其因为彼得蓄起了略长的头发、粗声粗气的做派和一根手杖，把他那个角色装扮得如此出色——逢有利奇菲尔德来的外乡人，便把他指点为「本城首富之一」，把他选进一切市政委员会，议论他半年一度的痛饮纵乐，议论他同几个体面人家「不认得」的女人的金钱瓜葛。而如今的这位布拉格登太太，看上去也相当知足。

===Original===
And as Stella loved him—
===Chinese===
而斯特拉既然爱过他——

===Original===
Well, as it was, Peter was then off on his honeymoon, and there was only I to bring the daffodils to Stella. She was always vain, was Stella; it would have grieved her had no one remembered.
===Chinese===
唉，实际情况是，彼得那时正出门度蜜月，把黄水仙送到斯特拉跟前的，便只剩我一个人。斯特拉这个人，从来是虚荣的；倘若竟没有一个人记得她，她是要伤心的。

### III / 三

===Original===
Then I caught the afternoon train for Fairhaven, and went back to my capable fiancée.
===Chinese===
随后我搭下午的火车回了费尔黑文，回到我那位能干的未婚妻身边。

===Original===
But I walked over to Willoughby Hall that night and found Charteris alone in his queer library, among the serried queer books and the portraits of his “literary creditors.” When I came into the apartment he was mending a broken teacup, for he peculiarly delighted in such infinitesimal task-work; but the vexed countenance at once took on the fond young look my coming would invariably provoke, and he shoved aside the fragments. …
===Chinese===
可当天夜里，我步行去了威洛比庄园，见查特里斯独自待在他那间古怪的书房里，四下环着他的，是密密匝匝的古怪书籍，还有他那一帧帧「文学债主」的画像。我进屋的时候，他正修补一只破裂的茶杯，因为他对这类纤毫毕现的细碎活计别有一种偏爱；可那张烦恼的脸霎时间换上了一副深情而年轻的神色——我一到场便总免不了引出这一副来——随即他把那些碎片推到了一旁。……

===Original===
We talked of trifles; apropos of nothing, Charteris said, “Yes—but, then, I devoted the morning to drawing up my will.” And I laughed over such forethought.
===Chinese===
我们聊了些琐碎事；忽然没头没脑地，查特里斯说：「对了——不过，我今天一上午，都用在立我的遗嘱上了。」我笑他有这份先见之明。

===Original===
The man rose and with clenched fist struck upon the littered table. “It is in the air. I swear to you that, somehow, *I* have been warned. But always I have been favoured—Why, man, I protest that never in my life have I encountered any person in associating with whom I did not condescend, with reason to back me! Yet today Death stands within arm’s reach, and I have accomplished—some three or four little books! And yet—why, ‘Ashtaroth’s Lackey,’ now—Yes, by God! it is perfected speech such as few other men have ever written. I know it, and I do not care at all even though you piteous dullards should always lack the wit to recognise and revere perfected speech when it confronts you. But presently I die! and there is nothing left of me save the inefficient testimony of those three or four little books!”
===Chinese===
他站起身，攥紧拳头，往那张狼藉一片的桌上一擂。「这兆头就弥漫在空气里。我向你起誓，不知怎么，*我*是得到过预警的。可我一向蒙受着偏爱——嗐，老兄，我敢说，我生平结交过的人里头，就没有哪一位，同他往来时我不曾屈尊俯就，而且理由十足！然而今天，死亡已站在一臂之遥，而我成就了的——不过三四本小书！然而——嗐，就说《亚斯他录的跟班》（Ashtaroth’s Lackey）吧——是的，上帝作证！那是炉火纯青的文字，旁的男人写得出的没有几个。这我心里清楚；哪怕你们这帮可怜的蠢材，撞见了炉火纯青的文字，永远缺那份认得出、敬得起的头脑，我也全然不在乎。可是转眼我就要死了！而留在身后的我，除了那三四本小书无力的见证之外，什么也不剩了！」

===Original===
I patted his shoulder and protested he had overworked himself.
===Chinese===
我拍拍他的肩膀，一口咬定他是把自己劳碌过头了。

===Original===
“Eh, well,” he said, and with that easy laugh I knew of old; “in any event, I have been thinking for a whole two hours of my wife, and of how from the very beginning I have utilised her, and of how good and credulous she is, and of how happy I have made her—! For I have made her happy. That is the preposterous part of it—”
===Chinese===
「嗐，也罢，」他说，还带着我素来熟悉的那阵从容的笑，「无论如何，我整整两个钟头都在想我的太太，想我从一开头便是在利用她，想她是多么善良、多么轻信，又想我把她弄成了多幸福的一个女人——！因为我确确实实使她幸福了。这正是其中荒唐透顶的一节——」

===Original===
“Why, yes; Anne loves you very dearly. Oh, I think that everybody is irrationally fond of you, John. No, that is not a compliment, it is rather the reverse. It is simply an instance of what I have been brooding over all this afternoon—that we like people on account of their good qualities and love them on account of their defects. I honestly believe that the cornerstone of affection is the agreeable perception of our superiority in some one point, at least, to the beloved. And that is why so many people are fond of you, I think.”
===Chinese===
「哦，正是；安妮非常非常地爱你。嗐，依我看，人人对你都是毫无道理地喜爱，约翰。不，这可不是恭维，毋宁说恰恰相反。这不过是我今天一下午反复琢磨的那件事的一个例证——我们喜欢人，是因为人的长处；我们爱人，却是因为人的短处。我老老实实相信，感情的基石，是一种愉快的知觉：觉出自己至少在某一点上，比所爱的人高明。我想，正因如此，才有这么多的人喜爱你。」

===Original===
He laughed a little. “And *de te fabula*—Yet I would distinguish. You think me a futile person and not, as we will put it, a disastrously truthful person, and so on through the entire list of all those so-called vices which are really just a habit of not doing this or that particular thing. Well! it is no longer à la mode to talk about God—yet I must confess to an old-fashioned faith in our Author’s existence and even in His amiability. I believe He placed me in this colourful world, and that He is not displeased because I have spent therein some forty-odd years pleasurably. Then too I have not wasted that pleasure, I have philanthropically passed it on. I have bequeathed posterity the chance to spend an enjoyable half-hour or so over one or two little books. That is not much to claim, but it is something.”
===Chinese===
他微微一笑。「*这故事说的也正是你*（de te fabula，贺拉斯《讽喻诗》语）——不过，我要求有所甄别。你当我是个无用的人，当我——姑且这样说——不是个诚实得闯祸的人，如此等等，一路数下去，开出来的全是那一类所谓的恶德，而它们其实不过是没做这样、那样某一件事的习惯罢了。嗐！谈论上帝，如今已不时兴了——可我必须招认，对我们那位造物主的存在、甚至对他的和蔼可亲，我还抱着一股老式的信心。我相信，是他把我安置在这个五光十色的世界上；我在此间快快活活地过了四十来年，他并不见怪。再说，这份快活我也没有白白糟蹋，我是本着博爱精神把它传递下去的。我遗赠给后世一份机会，让他们捧着一两本小书，消磨上惬意的半个来钟头。这算不得什么了不得的功劳，可总归是一点东西。」

===Original===
John Charteris was talking to himself now.
===Chinese===
约翰·查特里斯这会儿已是在自言自语了。

===Original===
“Had I instead the daily prayers of seven orphans, or the proud consciousness of having always been afraid to do what I wanted to—which I take to be the universally accredited insurance of a blissful eternity—or even a whole half-column with portrait in the New York papers to indicate what a loss my premature demise had been to America—or actually all three together, say, to exhibit as the increment of this period, I honestly cannot imagine any of the more intelligent archangels lining up to cheer my entry into Paradise. I believe, however, that to be contented, to partake of the world’s amenities with moderation as a sauce, and to aggrieve no fellow-being, except in self-protection, and to make other people happy as often as you find it possible, is a recipe for living that will pass muster even in heaven. There you have my creed; and it may not be impeccable, but I believe in it.”
===Chinese===
「换作是我拥有七个孤儿的每日祷词，或者拥有那份骄傲的意识——自己从来不敢做想做的事，我领会，这便是举世公认、保得永世至福的那张保险单——或者哪怕在纽约各报上连同肖像刊出整整半栏，昭示我的早夭对美国是何等损失——或者干脆三样凑齐，一并陈列，充作这段岁月的利息，我也老老实实想象不出，那班更有头脑的天使长里，有哪一位肯排起队来，为我进天国而喝彩。不过我倒是相信：知足常乐；把尘世的种种清福当佐料，有节制地享用；不亏待任何同类，除非出于自卫；还有，凡力所能及，便叫旁人快活——这样一张过日子的方子，拿到天国里去也经得起查验。这便是我的信条；它未必无可挑剔，可我信它。」

===Original===
“You have forgotten something,” I said, with a grin. “ ‘One must not think too despondently nor too often of the grim Sheriff who arrives anon to dispossess you, no less than all the others, nor of any subsequent and unpredictable legal adjustments.’ See, here it is, your own words printed in the book.”
===Chinese===
「你可忘了一样东西，」我咧嘴笑道。「『对那位严峻的执法官，切不可想得过于颓丧、过于频繁——他转眼便到，要把你、也把其余众人一概逐出各自占着的产业——对随后种种无从逆料的法律上的调整，也不可多想。』瞧，就在这儿，你自己的话，白纸黑字印在书里。」

===Original===
“Dear me, did I say that? How nicely phrased it is! Well! you and I have defiantly preserved the gallant attitude in an era not very favorable thereto. And we seem to prosper—as yet—”
===Chinese===
「哎呀，我说过这话么？措辞多漂亮！嗐！你和我，硬是在一个不大利于此的时代里，把这份骑士风度给保全了下来。而且我们看去还挺兴旺——至今为止——」

===Original===
“But certainly! We are the highly exceptional round pegs that flourish like green bay-trees in a square hole,” I summed it up. “Presently of course our place knoweth us not. But in the mean while—well, as it happens, I was recalling today how adroitly I scaled the summit of human wisdom when I was only fourteen. For I said then, ‘You can have a right good time first, anyway, if you keep away from ugly things and fussy people.’ And at twenty-five I stick to it.”
===Chinese===
「可不是嘛！我们便是那批绝无仅有的圆榫头，插在方榫眼里，偏长得像青翠的月桂树一般枝繁叶茂（语出《诗篇》「如青翠树旺茂」），」我给它下了总结。「当然，转眼之间，我们的原处便不再认得我们了（语出《约伯记》）。可是在此期间——嗐，说来也巧，我今天正好回想起，我十四岁上便何等利索地攀上了人类智慧的峰顶。因为那时我便说过：『只要躲开丑陋的东西和大惊小怪的人，你横竖总能先痛痛快快过上一阵好日子。』到二十五岁，我依旧咬定这条道理。」

===Original===
“I wonder now if it is not at a price?” said Charteris, rather mirthlessly. “Either way, you have as yet the courage of the unconvicted. And you have managed, out of it all, to get together the makings of an honest book. I do not generally believe in heaping flattery upon young authors, but if I had written that last book of yours it would not grieve me. Even so, I wonder—? But it is dreary here, in this old house, with all my wife’s high-minded ancestors chilling the air. Come, let us concoct some curious sort of drink.”
===Chinese===
「只是我如今犯疑，这怕不是不付代价的吧？」查特里斯说，笑得并不怎么欢。「无论哪条路，你到底还保有未定罪者的那份胆气（谐 the courage of one's convictions——坚持己见之胆量——的双关）。而且你竟从这一切里头，攒齐了一本老诚实实的书的坯子。我平素不赞成对年轻作者滥堆恭维，可你那本新书倘若出自我手，我不会引为憾事。虽是这么说，我还是犯疑——？罢了，这老宅子里凄清得很，我太太那一列志行高洁的祖先，把满屋的空气都熏凉了。来，咱们去调制一种古怪的饮料。」

===Original===
I looked at him compassionately. “And have Bettie staying up to let me in and smelling it on me! You must be out of your head.”
===Chinese===
我怜悯地望着他。「还要叫贝蒂熬着夜等我回家开门、再从我身上闻出这股味儿来！你准是脑袋出毛病了。」

===Original===
And then Charteris laughed and derided me, and afterward we chatted for a good two hours—quite at random, and disposing of the most important subjects, as was our usage when in argument, in a half-sentence.
===Chinese===
于是查特里斯哈哈大笑，把我讥嘲了一番；随后我们信马由缰地聊了足足两个钟头——全然漫无边际，而最重大的题目，照我们两人论战时的老规矩，半句话便打发掉。

===Original===
It was excellent to have Charteris to talk against, and I enjoyed it. Taking him by and large, I loved the little fellow as I have loved no other man.
===Chinese===
有查特里斯在跟前作对谈之资，真是妙极了，我受用得很。大体而论，我爱这个小个子男人，胜过我生平爱过的任何男人。

## XXX / 三十

He Gilds the Weathervane / 他为风信鸡镀金

### I / 一

===Original===
But I would not go along with Charteris the next morning when he came by the Hamlyns’ on his way to King’s College. I could not, because I was labouring over a batch of proof-sheets; and as I laboured my admiration for the very clever young man who had concocted this new book augmented comfortably; so that I told Charteris he was a public nuisance, and please to go to Tillietudlem.
===Chinese===
可是第二天早晨，查特里斯上国王学院去、顺路到哈姆林家来约我同行，我却不愿陪他去。也不能去，因为我正伏案对付一大摞校样；而伏案的工夫，我对炮制出这本新书的那个聪明绝顶的年轻人，敬意便舒舒服服地与日俱增；于是我告诉查特里斯，他是一宗公害，并请他这就上蒂利图德勒姆去吧（Tillietudlem，司各特《修墓老人》中的城堡名，时人借作「下地狱去」的雅谑）。

===Original===
He had procured the key to the Library﻿—for the College had not opened as yet﻿—and meant to borrow an odd volume or so of Lucian. Charteris had evolved the fantastic notion of treating Lucian’s Zeus as a tragic figure. He sketched a sympathetic picture of the fallen despot, and of the smokeless altars, girdled by a jeering rabble of so-called philosophers, and of how irritating it must be to anybody to have your actual existence denied. Did I not see the pathos of poor Zeus’s situation with the god business practically “cornered,” and the Jews getting all the trade?
===Chinese===
他弄到了图书馆的钥匙——学院还没有开学——打算借上一两册琉善（Lucian）的散本。查特里斯琢磨出了一个异想天开的念头：要把琉善笔下的宙斯写成一个悲剧人物。他勾勒出一幅令人动怜的图景：那位失势的君王，祭坛上断了香火，被一群讪笑不休、自命为哲学家的乌合之众所环伺；又勾出这样一桩任谁都要恼火的事：自己的实际存在竟被人一口否认。神道这门行当眼看叫人「包了圆」、买卖全落进犹太人手里——可怜的宙斯落到这般田地，其中的凄凉意味，我难道看不出来么？

===Original===
I informed him that the only pathos in life just at present was my inability to disprove, in default of abolishing, the existence of people who bothered me when I was busy. So Charteris went away, just as Byam brought the mail from the post-office.
===Chinese===
我告诉他，目下人生中唯一的凄凉意味便是：那些趁我忙碌时来搅扰的人，我废除不掉他们的存在，退而求其次，连证明他们并不存在也办不到。于是查特里斯走了，恰好拜亚姆从邮局取邮件回来。

### II / 二

===Original===
There were two cheques from magazines. Life was very pleasant, in a quiet uneventful world. The *Fairhaven Gazette* for the week had come, too, to indicate that, as usual, nothing of grave import was happening in an agreeably monotonous world. True, the Bulgarians were issuing an appeal to civilization on the ground that they objected to being massacred, and cyclones were wrecking towns and killing quite a number of persons in Florida, and the strikes in Colorado were leading to divers homicides; but in Fairhaven these things did not seem to matter. And so the front page of the *Gazette* was, rightfully, reserved for Plans of the College for the Session of 1903–4. …
===Chinese===
有两张杂志寄来的支票。生活在一个宁静无事的世界上，很是惬意。本周的《费尔黑文公报》也到了，昭示一如既往：在这个可喜地单调的世界里，没有发生任何重大的事情。诚然，保加利亚人正向文明世界发出呼吁，理由是他们不愿意惨遭屠戮；龙卷风正在佛罗里达毁城毙人，死伤颇为可观；科罗拉多的罢工正闹出形形色色的人命官司；可是在费尔黑文，这些事似乎都无关痛痒。于是《公报》的头版，理所当然地留给了「学院 1903–4 学年诸项计划」。……

===Original===
I looked again. The President was explaining that he had intended no discourtesy to Sir Thomas Lipton by declining to attend the Seawanhaka-Corinthian Yacht Club dinner; Major Delmar had failed to beat Lou Dillon’s time, on the same track; the National Dressmakers’ Association had declared that the kangaroo walk and Gibson shoulders would shortly be eschewed by all really fashionable women; and these matters were more interesting, of course, but certainly no cause for excitement. Well, I reflected, no news was good news proverbially; and I was content to let the axiom pass.
===Chinese===
我又看了一眼。校长正在解释：他谢绝出席西瓦纳卡-科林斯游艇俱乐部的晚宴，对托马斯·立顿爵士实无半分不敬之意；德尔马少校在同一跑道上，仍未能跑赢卢·狄龙创下的纪录；全国女服裁缝协会已经宣布：凡真正时髦的女性，不久都将摒弃袋鼠步态与吉布森肩膀；这些事情自然更有意趣，却断然算不上激动的理由。好吧，我心想，俗话说的，没有消息便是好消息；这条公理，我也乐得放它过关。

===Original===
In fine, there was nothing to worry over anywhere. And the book was going to be good, quite astonishingly good. …
===Chinese===
总之，天下没有任何一处有一桩事值得操心。而且这本书会是好书，好得惊人。……

===Original===
And yonder Bettie waited for me, and I could hear the piano that proclaimed she was not idle. I was ineffably content; and at ease within a rather kindly universe, taking it by and large. …
===Chinese===
而那一边，贝蒂正等着我；我听得见琴声，昭告她并没有闲着。我惬意得无以言表；置身于一个相当和善的宇宙之内，大体而论，安然自适。……

===Original===
“Quite a nice Setebos, after all! a big, fine generous-hearted fellow, who doesn’t bother to keep accounts to the last penny. I heartily approve of Setebos, and Bettie ought not to rag Him so. She would think it tremendously nice and boyish of me if I were to go impulsively and tell her something like that﻿—”
===Chinese===
「说到底，塞特博斯到底是位好样的神！一个高大、体面、心肠宽厚的家伙，懒得把账目清算到最后一文钱。我衷心赞许塞特博斯，贝蒂不该那样揶揄祂。倘若我突然一阵冲动，跑过去对她说上这么一番话，她准会觉得我这人妙极了、孩子气得可爱——」

===Original===
So I decided I had worked quite long enough.
===Chinese===
于是我断定，我的工夫已经下得够久的了。

### III / 三

===Original===
But as I reached out toward the portieres, a man came into the room, entering from the hallway. And I gave a little whistling sound of astonishment and hastened to him with extended hand.
===Chinese===
可是正当我伸手去掀门帘，一个男人从穿堂那头走进屋来。我轻轻打了一声唿哨，以示惊奇，赶忙迎上去，伸出了手。

===Original===
“My dear fellow,” I began; “why, have you dropped from the moon?”
===Chinese===
「我亲爱的伙计，」我开口道，「嗐，你是从月亮上掉下来的不成？」

===Original===
“They﻿—they told me you were here,” said Jasper Hardress, and paused to moisten his lips. “My wife died, yonder in Montana, ten days ago last Thursday﻿—yes, it was on a Tuesday she died, I think.”
===Chinese===
「他们——他们告诉我你在这儿，」贾斯珀·哈德雷斯说，顿了顿，润了润嘴唇。「我的妻子死了，就在那边的蒙大拿——十天前的上个星期四——对，我想她是死在一个星期二的。」

===Original===
And I was silent for a breathing-space. “Yes?” I said, at last; for I had seen the shining thing in Jasper Hardress’s hand, and I was wondering now why he had pocketed the toy, and for how long.
===Chinese===
我沉默了喘一口气的工夫。「是么？」我终于开口；因为我早已看见贾斯珀·哈德雷斯手里那件亮闪闪的东西，此刻我正在纳闷，他方才为什么把那件玩意儿揣回了口袋，又已经揣了多久。

===Original===
“It was of a fever she died. She was delirious﻿—oh, quite three days. And she talked in her delirium.”
===Chinese===
「她是害热病死的。她一直发着谵语——哦，足足有三天。她在谵语里说了好些话。」

===Original===
I began to smile; it was like witnessing a play. “Yonder is Bettie and my one chance of manhood; and blind chance, just the machination of a tiny microbe, entraps me as I tread toward all this. I was wrong about Setebos. Heine was right; there is an Aristophanes in heaven.”
===Chinese===
我开始露出笑意；这就像在看一出戏。「那一边是贝蒂，是我赢得成人品格的唯一机缘；而盲目的机运——凭的不过是一个小小微生物的奸谋——正当我举步走向这一切的当口，把我一举擒获。我把塞特博斯看错了。海涅说得对；天上坐着一位阿里斯托芬。」

===Original===
I said, aloud: “Well, Hardress, you wouldn’t have me dispute the veracity of a lady?”
===Chinese===
我出声说道：「嗐，哈德雷斯，你总不至于要我来质疑一位女士话里的真实性吧？」

===Original===
But the man did not appear to hear me. “Oh, it was very horrible,” he said. “Oh, I would like you, first of all, to comprehend how horrible it was. She was always calling﻿—no, not calling exactly, but just moaning one name, and over and over again. He had been so cruel, she said. He didn’t really care for anything, she said, except to write his hateful books. And I had loved her, you understand. And for three whole days I must sit there and hear her tell of what another man had meant to her! I have not been wholly sane, I think, since then, for I had loved her for a long time. And her throat was so little that I often thought how easy it would be to stop the moaning and talking, but somehow I did not like to do it. And it isn’t my honour that I mean to avenge. It is Gillian that I must avenge﻿—Gillian who died because a coward had robbed her of the will to live. For it was that in chief. Why, even you must understand that,” he said, as though he pleaded with me.
===Chinese===
可是这人仿佛没有听见我。「哦，那情形可怕得很，」他说。「哦，我头一件心愿，便是要你明白那情形有多可怕。她总是在呼唤——不，算不上呼唤，只是呻吟着一个名字，一遍又一遍。他从前待她何其残忍，这是她说的。他对世上任何东西都不真正上心，这也是她说的，除了写他那些可恨的书。而我爱过她，这你是知道的。整整三天，我必须坐在那里，听她讲述另一个男人于她意味着什么！打那以后，我想我就不曾全然清醒过，因为我爱她爱了很久很久。她的喉咙那么细，我常常想，要止住那呻吟和诉说该是何等容易，可不知怎么的，我不愿意那样做。再说，我要报仇雪恨的，并不是我的名誉。我必须为之复仇的是吉莲——是吉莲，她之所以死，是因为一个懦夫夺走了她活下去的意志。首要的根由，正在这一层。嗐，连你也必定懂得的，」他说，那口气像是在向我求告。

===Original===
And yonder Bettie played﻿—with lithe fingers which caressed the keys rather than struck them, I remembered. And always at the back of my mind some being that was not I was taking notes as to how unruffled the man was; and I smiled a little, in recognition of the air, as Bettie began “The Funeral March of a Marionette.” …
===Chinese===
而那一边，贝蒂弹着琴——用的是柔韧的手指，与其说是敲击琴键，不如说是爱抚琴键，这我记得。而我心智的背后，始终有某一个并非我的存在，在一五一十地记录着这人何等不动声色；我微微一笑，认出了这支曲调——贝蒂弹起来了，弹的是《木偶的葬礼进行曲》。……

===Original===
“Yes,” I said; “I think I understand. There is something to be advanced upon the other side perhaps; but that scarcely matters. You act within your rights; and, besides, you have a pistol, and I haven’t. I am getting afraid, though, Jasper. I can’t stand this much longer. So for God’s sake, make an end of this!”
===Chinese===
「是，」我说，「我想我明白。另一面或许也有些话可讲；但那算不得要紧。你行事并未逾越自己的权利；何况你有手枪，我没有。不过，贾斯珀，我渐渐害怕起来了。这样的滋味我再也受不下去了。看在上帝份上，快把这一场了结了吧！」

===Original===
Jasper Hardress said: “I mean to. But they told me he was here? Yes, I am sure that someone told me he was here.”
===Chinese===
贾斯珀·哈德雷斯说：「我正有此意。可是他们不是告诉我他在这儿么？是啊，我敢肯定有人对我说过，他在这儿。」

===Original===
I think I must have reeled a little. I know my brain was working automatically. Gillian Hardress had always called me Jack; and Jasper Hardress was past reason; and yonder was Bettie, who had made life too fine and dear a thing to be relinquished. …
===Chinese===
我想我当时必定晃了一晃。我知道我的脑子在自动运转。吉莲·哈德雷斯生前一直管我叫杰克；而贾斯珀·哈德雷斯已不可理喻；而那一边是贝蒂——她已把生命造成一件过于美好、过于可贵的东西，教人再舍不得撒手。……

===Original===
“Jasper,” someone was saying, and that someone seemed to laugh, “we aren’t living in the Middle Ages, remember. No, just as I said, I cannot stand this nonsense any longer, and you must make an end of this foolishness. Just on a bare suspicion﻿—just on the ravings of a delirious woman﻿—! Why, she used to call *me* Jack﻿—and I write books﻿—Why, you might just as logically murder *me*!”
===Chinese===
「贾斯珀，」有什么人在说话，而那什么人似乎在笑，「咱们可不是活在中世纪，别忘了。不，正像我方才说的，这种胡闹我再也受不下去了，你必须把这场蠢事了结掉。仅凭一丝空空的怀疑——仅凭一个谵妄妇人的呓语——！嗐，她从前不是也管*我*叫杰克么——我也是写书的人——嗐，照这么讲，你把*我*杀掉，岂不同样合乎逻辑！」

===Original===
“I thought at first it was you. Oh, only for a moment, boy. I was not quite sane, I think, for at first I suspected you of such treachery as in my sober senses I know you never dreamed of. And I had forgotten you were just a child﻿—But she was conscious at the end,” said Jasper Hardress, “and when I﻿—talked with her about what she had said in delirium, she told me it was Charteris whose son we christened Jasper Hardress some two years ago﻿—”
===Chinese===
「起初我还当那是你。哦，不过一时的念头，孩子。我想我当时不大清醒，因为我起初竟疑心你干出了那等背信弃义的事——凭我清醒时的理智，我明知你连做梦也梦不见那种事。何况我还忘了，你不过是个孩子——可她临终的时候是清醒的，」贾斯珀·哈德雷斯说，「等我——同她问起她谵语里说的那些话，她告诉我，大约两年前我们行洗礼取名贾斯珀·哈德雷斯的那个儿子，是查特里斯的——」

===Original===
I said: “I never knew there was a child.” But I was thinking of a hitherto unaccounted-for photograph.
===Chinese===
我说：「我从不知道有这么个孩子。」但我心里转的，是一张先前始终无从索解的照片。

===Original===
“He only lived three months. I had always wanted a son. You cannot fancy how proud I was of him.” Hardress laughed here.
===Chinese===
「他只活了三个月。我一直盼着有个儿子。你想象不出，我当时为他多么骄傲。」说到这里，哈德雷斯笑了。

===Original===
“And she told you it was Charteris! in the moment of death when﻿—when you were threatening me, she told you it was Charteris!”
===Chinese===
「而她临死的那一刻竟告诉你，那是查特里斯！就在那个当口——就在你威吓着我的时候，她告诉你，那是查特里斯！」

===Original===
“It is different when you are dying. You see﻿—Gillian knew that eternity depended on what she said to me then﻿—” He spoke as with difficulty, and he kept licking at restless lips.
===Chinese===
「人到将死，情形便不同了。你要明白——吉莲知道，她永恒的归宿，就系于她那时对我说的话——」他说得颇为艰难，舌头不住地舔着焦躁不安的双唇。

===Original===
“Yes﻿—she did believe that. And she told you﻿—!” I comprehended how Gillian Hardress had loved me, and my shame was such that now it was the mere brute will to live which held me. But it held me, none the less. Besides, I saw the least unpleasant solution.
===Chinese===
「是——她的确信这一层。而她竟告诉了你——！」我这才懂得吉莲·哈德雷斯是怎样爱过我；我的羞惭之深，使得此刻攥住我不放的，只剩那一点纯粹的、畜生般的求生意志。可它到底还是攥住了我。况且，我已经瞅出了其中最不难堪的一条出路。

===Original===
“I suppose I can’t blame you,” I said﻿—“for if she told you, why, of course﻿—” Then I barked out: “He was here a moment ago. You must have come around one corner, in fact, just as he turned the other. You will find him at Willoughby Hall, I suppose. He said he was going straight home.”
===Chinese===
「我想我怪不得你，」我说——「因为既然是她告诉你的，嗐，那自然——」随即我粗声嚷道：「他方才还在这里。实际上，你准是从一个街角绕过来，恰好他从另一头拐了过去。我想，你去威洛比庄园准能找到他。他说过，他这就直接回家。」

===Original===
For I knew that Charteris was at King’s College, a mile away from Willoughby Hall; and, I assured myself, there would be ample time to warn him. Only how much must now depend upon the diverting qualities of Lucian! For should the Samosatan flag in interest, John would be leaving the College presently; and there is but one street in Fairhaven.
===Chinese===
因为我知道，查特里斯此刻正在国王学院，离威洛比庄园一英里之遥；而且我宽慰自己，赶去报信的时间尽够充裕。只是如今，一切都要看琉善引人的本领了！因为倘若这位萨莫萨塔人的书趣味一淡，约翰转眼就要离开学院；而费尔黑文统共只有一条街。

### IV / 四

===Original===
I had my hand upon the garden-gate, and Hardress had just turned the corner below, going toward Cambridge Street, when Bettie came upon the porch.
===Chinese===
我的手才搭上花园的门，哈德雷斯刚拐过下面的街角、朝剑桥街去了，贝蒂便来到了廊前。

===Original===
“Well,” she said, “and who’s your fat friend, Mr. Sheridan?”
===Chinese===
「喂，」她说，「你那位胖朋友是谁呀，谢里丹先生？（谢里丹为十八世纪英国喜剧家，此语借剧作家口吻打趣）」

===Original===
“I can’t stop now, dear. I forgot to tell John about something which is rather important﻿—”
===Chinese===
「这会儿可停不得，亲爱的。我忘了告诉约翰一桩相当要紧的事——」

===Original===
“Gracious!” Bettie Hamlyn said; “that sounds like shooting. Why, it is shooting, isn’t it?”
===Chinese===
「天哪！」贝蒂·哈姆林说，「那声音像是放枪。嗐，可不就是放枪么？」

===Original===
“Yes,” said I.
===Chinese===
「是，」我说。

===Original===
“﻿—Quite as though the Monnachins and the Massawomeks and all the other jawbreakers were attacking Fairhaven as they used to do on alternate Thursdays, and affording both of us an excellent opportunity to get nicely scalped in time for dinner. So I don’t mind confessing that it was against precisely such an emergency I declined to turn out an elaborate suite of hair; and now I expect the world at large to acknowledge that I acted very sensibly.”
===Chinese===
「——倒活像是莫纳钦人、马萨沃梅克人，连同其余一切名字拗口的部落，又照他们隔周四进犯一回的老规矩杀奔费尔黑文来了，给你我二人一个大好机会，赶在晚饭之前，齐齐整整地被剥掉头皮。所以我不妨坦白承认：正是为着防备这一类急变，我才没肯铺张出一套繁复的发式；如今我就指望世人众口一词地承认，我行事极有见地。」

===Original===
“It is much more likely to be some drunken countryman on his monthly spree﻿—” I was reflecting while Bettie talked nonsense that there had been no less than four shots. I was wondering whom the last was for. It would be much pleasanter, all around, if Hardress had sent it into his own disordered brain. Yes, certainly, three bullets ought amply to account for an unprepared and unarmed and puny Charteris. …
===Chinese===
「十之八九，不过是哪个乡下人在赶他每月一度的酒疯——」贝蒂胡说八道的当口，我心里盘算的是：方才枪响，少说有四声。我在琢磨，最后那一声是给谁预备的。倘若哈德雷斯把它送进了自己那颗错乱的脑袋，各方面都要痛快得多。是啊，肯定的，三颗子弹，对付一个毫无防备、手无寸铁、又瘦又小的查特里斯，想必绰绰有余了。……

===Original===
So I said: “Well, I suppose my business with John must wait for a while. Besides, Bettie, you are such a dear in that getup. And if you will come down into the garden at once, I will explain a few of my reasons for advancing the assertion.”
===Chinese===
于是我说：「嗐，我想我找约翰的那桩事，只好暂且等一等了。再说，贝蒂，你这身穿戴实在惹人怜爱。你若肯立刻下到花园里来，我就解释几条理由，说明我何以要提出这一项论断。」

===Original===
Standing upon the porch, she patted me ever so lightly upon the head. “What a child it is!” she said. “I don’t think that, after all, I shall put twenty-six candles on your cake next week. The fat and lazy literary gent is not really old enough, not really more than ten.”
===Chinese===
她站在廊前，极轻极轻地拍了拍我的头顶。「真是个孩子！」她说。「我想，归根到底，下星期我毕竟不会往你蛋糕上插二十六根蜡烛了。这位又胖又懒的文人先生，实在没有这么大年纪，实在满打满算不过十岁。」

===Original===
“﻿—And besides, apart from the proposed discussion of your physical charms, I have something else quite equally important to tell you about.”
===Chinese===
「——再说，除了方才提议的、关于你身上种种妙处的讨论之外，我另有一桩同样要紧的事，要讲给你听。」

===Original===
“Oh, drat the pertinacious infant, then I’ll come for half an hour. Just wait until I get a hat. Still, what a worthless child it is! to be quitting work before noon.”
===Chinese===
「嗐，这个缠人不休的小东西，那我就来半个钟头吧。且等我取一顶帽子。只是话说回来，这孩子多么没出息！还没到晌午，就撂下活计不干了。」

===Original===
And she would have gone, but I detained her. “Yes, what a worthless child it is﻿—or rather, what an unproverbial sort of busy bee it has been, Bettie dear. For his has been the summer air, and the sunshine, and the flowers; and gentle ears have listened to him, and gentle eyes have been upon him. Now it is autumn. And he has let others eat his honey﻿—which I take to include all that he actually made, all that wasn’t in the world before he came, as Stella used to say﻿—so that he might have his morsel and his song. And sometimes it has been Sardinian honey, very bitter in the mouth﻿—and even then he has let others eat it﻿—”
===Chinese===
她正要走，我却把她留住了。「是啊，多么没出息的一个孩子——或者不如说，亲爱的贝蒂，他从来是一头多么不合谚语脾性的忙碌蜜蜂。因为分派到他名下的，一向是夏日的空气、阳光和花朵；温柔的耳朵倾听过他，温柔的眼睛注视过他。如今是秋天了。而他听凭旁人吃他的蜜——依我想来，这蜜包括他真正酿出的一切，包括他来到世上之前世上还不曾有的一切，正如斯特拉从前常说的那样——为的是换来他自己的一口食物、一支歌。而有的时候，那是撒丁岛的蜜，入口极苦——即便是那时候，他也照样让旁人吃了——」

===Original===
“You are a most irrelevant infant,” said Miss Hamlyn, “with these insectean divagations﻿—Dear me, what lovely words! And of course if you really want to drag me into that baking-hot garden, and have the only fiancée you just at present possess laid up by a sunstroke﻿—”
===Chinese===
「你真是个离题万里的婴孩，」哈姆林小姐说，「满口这些昆虫学的浪游之谈——哎呀，多漂亮的字眼！当然啦，你要是当真存心把我拽进那座烤炉般的花园，好叫你眼下仅有的这一位未婚妻中了暑、困在病榻上——」

===Original===
To

			**Gabrielle Brooke Moncure**

Plus sapit vulgus, quia tantum, quantum opus est, sapit.
===Chinese===
献给

		**加布里埃尔·布鲁克·蒙丘尔（Gabrielle Brooke Moncure）**

庸众反而更有智慧，因为他只懂得需要懂得的那么多。（Plus sapit vulgus, quia tantum, quantum opus est, sapit.）

## The Epilogue / 尾声

Which Suggests That Second Thoughts﻿— / 略示再思——

===Original===
So I waited there alone. Whatever the four shots implied, I must tell Bettie everything, because she was Bettie, and it was not fair I should have any secrets from her. “Oh, just be honest with me,” she had said, in this same garden, “and I don’t care what you do!” And I had never lied to Bettie: at worst, I simply had not told her anything concerning matters about which I was glad she had not happened to ask any questions. But this was different.﻿ ﻿…

Dimly I knew that everything must pivot on my telling Bettie. John was done for, the Hardress woman was done for, and whether or no Jasper had done for himself, there was no danger, now, that anyone would ever know how that infernal Gillian had badgered me into, probably, three homicides. There might be some sort of supernal bookkeeping, somewhere, but very certainly it was not conformable to any human mathematics.﻿ ﻿… And therefore I must tell Bettie.

I must tell Bettie, and abide what followed. She had pardoned much. It might be she would pardon even this, “because I had been honest with her when I didn’t want to be.” And in any event﻿—even in her loathing﻿—Bettie would understand, and know I had at least kept faith with her.﻿ ﻿…

I must tell Bettie, and abide what followed. For living seemed somehow to have raised barriers about me a little by a little, so that I must view and talk with all my fellows more and more remotely, and could not, as it were, quite touch anybody save Bettie. At all other persons I was but grimacing falsely across an impalpable barrier. And now just such a barrier was arising between Bettie and me, as I perceived in a sort of panic. Yes, it was rising resistlessly, like an augmenting mist not ever to be put aside, except by plunging forthwith into hours, or days, or even into months perhaps, of ugliness and discomfort.﻿ ﻿…
===Chinese===
于是我独自在那里等候。那四声枪响无论意味着什么，我都必须把一切都告诉贝蒂——因为她是贝蒂，而我若对她还存着什么秘密，便不公道了。「哦，只消对我诚实，」她曾在这同一座花园里说过，「那样你做什么我都不在乎！」而我也从不曾对贝蒂撒谎：往坏里说，我不过是绝口不提某些事情罢了——那些事她不曾恰好问起，我还正自庆幸。可这一回不一样。……

我隐隐约约地知道，一切的一切，都系于我是否把真相告诉贝蒂。约翰完了，哈德雷斯家那个女人也完了；而无论贾斯珀是否也亲手结果了自己，如今都已毫无危险，再不会有任何人知道，那个可恶的吉莲是怎样死缠烂打，逼得我——多半——害下了三条人命。冥冥之中或许自有某种天上的簿记，但它断然不依从人间的任何算术。……所以，我必须告诉贝蒂。

我必须告诉贝蒂，并承受随之而来的一切。她宽恕过许多。说不定连这一桩她也会宽恕——「因为我本来不想坦白，却还是对她坦白了」。而且无论如何——哪怕她厌恶已极——贝蒂总会明白，总会知道我至少守住了对她的信义。……

我必须告诉贝蒂，并承受随之而来的一切。因为生活仿佛已经一点一点地在我四周筑起了壁垒，害得我不得不与我的一切同类越来越隔膜地相望、交谈，而且可以说，除了贝蒂，我简直触碰不到任何人。对其余所有的人，我不过是隔着一场无形的屏障，装腔作态地扮着一副假面。而如今，恰恰是这样一道屏障，正在贝蒂与我之间升起——这一点，我怀着几分惊惶看得分明。是的，它正无可抗拒地升起来，像一场越积越浓、再也拨不开的雾，除非立刻一头扎进若干时辰、或者若干天、甚至也许若干个月的难堪与不适里去。……
===Original===
It was the season of harvest. The leaves were not yet turned, and upon my face the heatless, sun-steeped air was like a caress. The whole world was at full-tide, ineffably sweet and just a little languorous: and bees were audible, as in a humorous pretence of vexation.﻿ ﻿…

The world was very beautiful. I must tell Bettie presently, of course; only the world was such a comfortable place precisely as it was; and I began to wonder if I need tell Bettie after all?

For, after all, to tell the truth could resurrect nobody; and to know the truth would certainly make Bettie very unhappy; and never in my life have I been able to endure the contact of unhappiness.
===Chinese===
那时正是收获的时节。树叶还没有转色；浸透了阳光而不带一丝燠热的空气拂在我脸上，宛如爱抚。整个世界正涨满了潮，甜美得难以言传，还带一点慵懒：蜜蜂的嗡嗡声听得分明，仿佛在滑稽地佯装恼怒。……

世界真是美好。当然，我过一会儿就得把话告诉贝蒂；只是这世界恰照眼下这副模样，才正是这样一处舒服的所在；于是我渐渐开始犯疑：归根到底，究竟还有没有必要告诉贝蒂呢？

因为，归根到底，把真相说了出来，也复活不了任何人；而知道了真相，贝蒂却必定会很不快活；而我生平从来不曾忍受得了同不幸打照面。

## An Introduction / 导言

===Original===
By Wilson Follett

===Chinese===
威尔逊·福莱特（Wilson Follett）撰

===Original===
Mr. Cabell, in making ready this second or intended edition of *The Cords of Vanity*, performs an act of reclamation which is at the same time an act of fresh creation.

===Chinese===
卡贝尔先生在为《虚妄之绳》（The Cords of Vanity）筹备这第二个版本、亦即「拟定版」（Intended Edition）之际，所行的是一桩收复之举，而与此同时，也是一桩崭新的创造之举。

===Original===
For the purely reclamatory aspect of what he has done, his reward (so far as that can consist in anything save the doing) must come from insignificantly few directions; so few indeed that he, with a wrily humorous exaggeration, affects to believe them singular. The author of this novel has been pleased to describe the author of this introduction as “the only known purchaser of the book” and, further, as “the other person to own a *Cords of Vanity*.” I could readily enough acquit myself, with good sound legal proofs, of any such singularity as stands charged in this soft impeachment—and that without appeal to *The Cleveland Plain Dealer* of eleven years ago (“slushy and disgusting”), or to *The New York Post* (“sterile and malodorous … worse than immoral—dull”), or to *Ainslee’s Magazine* (“inconsequent and rambling … rather nauseating at times”). These devotees of the adjective that hunts in pairs are hardly to be discussed, I suppose, in connection with any rewards except such as accrue to the possessors of a certain obtuseness, who always and infallibly reap at least the reward of not being hurt by what they do not know—or, for that matter, by what they do know. He who writes such a book as *The Cords of Vanity* is committing himself to the supremely irrational faith that this dullness is somehow not the ultimate arbiter; and for him the pronouncements of this dullness simply do not figure among either his rewards or his penalties. So, it is not exactly to these tributes of the press that one reverts in noting that *The Cords of Vanity*, on its publication eleven years ago, promptly became a book which there were—almost—none to praise and very few to love. After all, its author’s computation of that former audience of his—his actual individual voluntary readers of a decade ago—appears to be but slightly and pardonably exaggerated on the more modest side of the fact. If there were a Cabell Club of membership determined solely by the number of those who, already possessing *The Cords of Vanity* in its first edition, recognize it as the work of a serious artist of high achievement and higher capacity, I suspect that the smallness of that club would be in inordinate disproportion to everything but its selectness and its members’ pride in “belonging.”

===Chinese===
至于他所做之事中纯然收复的那一面，他的报偿（倘若报偿竟能是实干之外的任何东西）只能来自少得不足道的几个方向；少到这般地步，以致他带着一种挖苦式的诙谐夸张，佯装相信它们独一无二。这位小说的作者曾不吝雅意，把本篇导言的作者描述为「已知的本书唯一购书人」，更进一步，描述为「拥有另一册《虚妄之绳》的另一个人」。我原不难为自己开脱，拿出扎实过硬的法律证据，证明这桩温文指控所控的这一类独一无二均不成立——而且无须援引十一年前的《克利夫兰实话报》（The Cleveland Plain Dealer，评曰「甜腻而恶心」），无须援引《纽约邮报》（The New York Post，「贫瘠而恶臭……比不道德更糟——乏味」），也无须援引《安斯利杂志》（Ainslee’s Magazine，「散漫无绪、枝枝蔓蔓……时而相当倒胃口」）。我想来，这班信奉「成双出猎的形容词」的人，是很难放到任何报偿之下来讨论的——除非是落到某种迟钝之所有者头上的那类报偿：这类人永远而且万无一失地至少有一份收成——不因自己不知道的东西而受伤，或者，就此而言，也不因自己知道的东西而受伤。写下《虚妄之绳》这样一本书的人，等于是把自己托付给了一种登峰造极的非理性信仰：相信这种乏味好歹不是最终的仲裁者；而对他来说，这种乏味发表的种种判词，根本就既不在他的报偿之列，也不在他的刑罚之列。所以，当人们指出《虚妄之绳》十一年前出版之际旋即沦为一本——几乎——无人赞、极少人爱的书时，心里真正萦回的，实在算不得报刊的这些颂词。毕竟，本书作者对自己昔日读者人数的估算——即对十年前他那些实际的、逐一可数的、自愿的读者的估算——比起事实来，看来只是朝着更谦虚的一头，作了些微而情有可原的夸大。倘若世上真有一个卡贝尔俱乐部，其会员人数全然取决于这样一批人的数目——他们既已拥有初版的《虚妄之绳》，又承认它出自一位成就已高、潜力更高的严肃艺术家之手——那么我疑心，这个俱乐部之小，将与一切都失了比例，唯独两点除外：它的精选，以及会员们以「身在其中」为荣的那份骄矜。

===Original===
Be that as it may, the economist-author, on the eve of his book’s emergence from the limbo of “out of print,” prefers that it come into its redemption carrying a foreword by someone who knew it without dislike in its former incarnation. No contingent liability, it seems, can dissuade Mr. Cabell from this preference. An author who once elected to precede a group of his best tales with an introduction eloquently setting forth reasons why the collection ought not to be published at all, is hardly to be deterred now by the mere inexpediency of hitching his star to a farm-wagon. His own graciously unreasonable insistence must be the excuse, such as it is, for the present introduction, such as it is. If there may be said to exist a sort of charter membership in Mr. Cabell’s audience, this document is to be construed as representing its very enthusiastic welcome to the later and vastly larger elective membership.

===Chinese===
无论如何，这位善于经济核算的作者，在他的书行将脱离「绝版」湮没之境的前夜，宁愿它走向赎回时随身携带一篇前言——执笔者须是一位在它前世化身中识得它而不曾心怀厌憎的人。看来，任何或有负债都劝不转卡贝尔先生放弃这层偏爱。一位曾主动为他一组最好的短篇小说冠以一篇导言、并在其中雄辩滔滔地陈述这部集子压根就不该出版的种种理由的作者，如今断不至于被「把他那颗星辰拴上一辆农庄马车」的区区失策吓退。他自己那番温文而不通情理的执拗，只好权充眼前这篇导言的托词——姑且算是托词；一如这篇导言，姑且算是导言。倘若可以说卡贝尔先生的读者群中存在着某种创始会员的话，那么本文应当被解读为：代表他们，向那批后来的、庞大得多的当选会员，致以十二分热忱的欢迎。

===Original===
And if, weighed as such a welcome, it proves hopelessly inadequate, at least it provides a number of possible compensations by the way. For instance, that *New York World* critic who damned the book but praised its frontispiece of 1909, has now a uniquely pat opportunity to balance his ledger by praising the book and damning this foreword, which, more or less, replaces the frontispiece. Similarly, the more renowned critic and anthologist who so well knows the “originals” of the verses in *From the Hidden Way*, can now render poetically perfect justice to all who will care by perceiving that both the earlier edition of this book and the author of this foreword are but figments of Mr. Cabell’s slightly puckish invention.

===Chinese===
而倘若放在这样一封欢迎辞的天平上称，它显得无可救药地斤两不足，那么它至少也沿途提供了若干可能的补偿。譬如，当年那位诅咒这本书、却称许它 1909 年版卷首插图的《纽约世界报》（New York World）评论家，如今便有了一个再顺手不过的机会，好轧平他的账簿：称赞这本书，而诅咒这篇前言——须知这篇前言，多多少少，正是那幅卷首插图的替身。同理，那位深知《来自幽径》（From the Hidden Way）集中诸诗「原型」底细的、更有名气的评论家兼选家，如今也可以向一切介意此事者赐下一份诗意上完美无缺的公道：只需识破——本书的先前版本也好，本篇前言的作者也好，统统不过是卡贝尔先生那略带促狭的虚构捏造出来的幻影罢了。

===Original===
But these pages must not be, like those which follow, a comedy of shirking. They will have flouted a plain duty unless they speak of the sense and the degree in which this novel, during the process of reclaiming it, has been actually recreated. Perhaps the matter can be packed most succinctly into the statement that Mr. Cabell’s hero has been subjected to such a process of growth as has made him commensurate in stature with the other two modern writers of Mr. Cabell’s invention. As *The Cream of the Jest* is essentially the book of Felix Kennaston and *Beyond Life* that of John Charteris, so *The Cords of Vanity* is essentially the book of Robert Etheridge Townsend. Now, this Townsend has accomplished a deal of growing since 1909. By this I do not mean that he is taken at a later period of his own imagined life, or that he fails to act consonantly with the extreme youth imputed to him: I mean that he is the creation of a more mature mind, a deeper philosophy, a more probing insight into the implications of things. A given youth of twenty-five will be very differently interpreted by an observer of thirty and by the same observer at forty, very much as a given era of the past will be understood differently by a single historian before and after certain cycles of his own social and political experience. The past never remains to us the same past; it grows up along with us; the physical facts may remain admittedly the same, but our understanding accents them differently, finds more in them at some points and less at others. So Robert Etheridge Townsend remains an example of that special temperament which, being unable to endure the contact of unhappiness, consistently shirks every responsibility that entails or threatens discomfort; and the truth about him, taking him as an example of just that temperament, is still inexorably told. But his weakness as a man becomes much more tolerable in this second version, because it is much more intimately and poignantly correlated with his strength as an artist. One is made to feel that he, like Charteris, may the better consummate in his art the auctorial virtues of distinction and clarity, beauty and symmetry, tenderness and truth and urbanity, precisely because his personal life is bereft of those virtues. Less than before, the accent is on the wastrel in Townsend; more than before, it is on the potential creator of beauty in him. The earlier readers will hardly count it as a fault that Mr. Cabell has contrived to make his novel, without detriment to any truth whatsoever, a far less unpleasant book. Sardonic it still is, by a necessary implication, but not wantonly, and with a mellowness. The irony, which at its harshest was capable of rasping the nerves, has become capable of wringing the heart.

===Chinese===
但眼下这几页，万万不可像随后的那几百页一样，沦为一出遁避的喜剧。它们必须谈到：这部小说在收复自身的过程中，究竟在何种意义上、到何种程度上，被实实在在地重新创造了——否则，它们便是公然玩忽了一项明白的义务。这层意思，或许可以最简洁地打包成一句话：卡贝尔先生笔下的主人公，经受了一番生长的过程，而这番生长，已使他在身量上与卡贝尔先生虚构的另外两位现代作家并驾齐驱。正如《玩笑的精华》（The Cream of the Jest）本质上是费利克斯·肯纳斯顿（Felix Kennaston）之书，《超越人生》（Beyond Life）本质上是约翰·查特里斯（John Charteris）之书，《虚妄之绳》在本质上，便是罗伯特·埃瑟里奇·汤森（Robert Etheridge Townsend）之书。而这位汤森，自 1909 年以来，着实生长了一大截。我这样说，并不是指他被置于其想象生平的更晚一段，也不是指他的行事与他被设定的极端年轻不相吻合：我是说，他如今是一个更成熟的心智、一套更深的哲学、一种对诸般事物之底蕴更具穿透力的洞察，所创造出来的人物。同样一个二十五岁的青年，在一位三十岁的观察者眼中，与在这同一位观察者四十岁时的眼中，会得到截然不同的诠释；这就非常像过去的某一个时代，会因同一位史家处在自身社会与政治经验的某些周期之前或之后，而得到不同的理解。过去从不曾为我们停留在同一个过去里；它与我们一同长大；种种物理事实尽可以照旧不变，但我们的理解落在它们上面的重音不同了，在有些点上从中读出更多，在另一些点上读出更少。所以，罗伯特·埃瑟里奇·汤森依旧是那样一种特殊气质的标本：这种气质受不住不幸的触碰，便一以贯之地遁避每一份会招致或威胁不适的责任；而关于他的真相——就把他取作恰恰这种气质的标本而言——依旧被不留情面地和盘托出。但在这第二个版本里，他作为人的软弱变得好受得多了，因为它与作为艺术家的他的力量，关联得远比从前内在，也远比从前锥心。读者被引导着去感到：他同查特里斯一样，恰恰因为个人生活中被剥夺了那些品德，反而能更好地在他的艺术里，把属于作者行当的诸般美德——卓异与明晰、美与匀称、温存与真实与温文——臻于圆成。比起从前，重音落在汤森身上浪子一面的，少了；比起从前，落在潜伏于他之中的那个美的创造者身上的，多了。早年的读者们，恐怕很难把下面这点算作过失：卡贝尔先生设法使他的小说——对任何一种真实都丝毫无损——变成了一本远不那么令人不快的书。它照旧讥诮，这是题中必然之义，但不再恣意妄为，而且添了一层温醇。那昔日最严苛时足以锉磨神经的反讽，如今已变得足以绞拧人心。

===Original===
Other reasons there are, too, for holding that *The Cords of Vanity* is certain to make its second appeal to a many times multiplied audience. Since divers momentous transactions of the years just gone, the whole world stands in a moral position extraordinarily well adapted to the comprehension of just such a comedy of shirking; and especially the world of thought has received a powerful impulsion toward the area long occupied by Mr. Cabell’s romantic pessimism. There is perhaps somewhat more demand for satire, or at least a growing toleration of it. Moreover, by sheer patience and reiteration Mr. Cabell has procured no little currency for some of his most characteristic ideas. Chivalry and gallantry, as he analyzes them, are concepts which play their part in the inevitable present re-editing of social and literary history. *The Rivet in Grandfather’s Neck*, *The Cream of the Jest*, and *The Certain Hour* have somewhat to say to the discriminating, even on other than purely aesthetic grounds; *Beyond Life* is on the threshold of its day as the *Sartor Resartus* of one side, the aesthetic side, of modernism;

“Of *Jurgen* eke they maken mencion”;

and *The Cords of Vanity* is but the first of the earlier books to be reissued in the format of the uniform and accessible Intended Edition.

===Chinese===
此外还有别的理由，叫人相信《虚妄之绳》势必会向一个翻了无数番的读者群发出第二次召唤。自刚刚逝去的几年间种种重大的变故以来，全世界所站的道德位置，已变得格外宜于领会恰是这样一出遁避的喜剧；思想界尤甚——它已被一股强大的推力，推向卡贝尔先生的浪漫悲观主义久已盘踞的那片领地。对讽刺的需求兴许多了一些，或者至少，对它的容忍在增长。况且，凭着十足的耐心与不厌其烦的重申，卡贝尔先生已为他若干最具个人印记的观念谋得了不小的流通。骑士精神与殷勤风致，经他这般剖析之后，已然成了一对概念，正在眼下这场社会史与文学史的必然重编之中各尽其分。《祖父颈上的铆钉》（The Rivet in Grandfather’s Neck）、《玩笑的精华》与《注定的时辰》（The Certain Hour），即便撇开纯审美的理由不谈，对眼光挑剔者也有些话要说；《超越人生》正站在它时代的门槛上，堪当现代主义一面——审美的一面——的《拼凑的裁缝》（Sartor Resartus）；

「于尤尔根（Jurgen），彼辈亦有称述」；

而《虚妄之绳》，不过是头一批以那统一齐整、唾手可得的「拟定版」版式重新面世的早期作品罢了。

===Original===
While *The Cords of Vanity* was out of print, a fresh copy is known to have been acquired for twenty-five cents. Copies of a more recent work by the same hand—a tale which has been rendered equally unavailable to the public, though by slightly different considerations—have fetched as much as one hundred times that sum. This arithmetic may be, in part, the gauge of an unsought and distasteful notoriety; but that very notoriety, by the most natural of transitions, will lead the curious on from what cannot be obtained to what can, and some who have begun by seeking one particular work of a great artist will end by discovering the artist. In short, it is rational to expect that the fortunes hereafter of this rewritten novel will very excellently illustrate the uses of adversity.

===Chinese===
《虚妄之绳》绝版期间，据知曾有人以二十五美分购得一本崭新的书。而同一只手笔下一部较近的作品——一部因由略有不同、同样变得无处可购的小说——其册本曾卖到过高达此数一百倍的价钱。这笔算术，部分地也许量出的是一桩不求而至、并不甘美的恶名；但恰恰是那桩恶名，会以最自然的路径，把好奇之徒从弄不到手的引向弄得到手的；而某些起初心心念念只想觅得一位伟大艺术家某一部特定作品的人，终将发现这位艺术家本身。简而言之，我们有理由预期：这部改写后的小说此后的际遇，将把逆境的妙用诠释得淋漓尽致。

===Original===
Not, I repeat, that any great part of the reward for such writing can come from without. According to Robert Etheridge Townsend, “a man writes admirable prose not at all for the sake of having it read, but for the more sensible reason that he enjoys playing solitaire”—a not un-Cabellian saying. And, even of the reward from without, it may be questioned whether the really indispensable part ever comes from the multitude. A lady with whose more candid opinions the writer of this is more frequently favored nowadays than of old has said: “Every time I hear of somebody who has wanted one of these books without being able to get it, or who, having got it, has conceded it nothing better than the disdain of an ignoramus, I feel as if I must forthwith get out the copy and read it through again and again, until I have read it once for every person who has rejected it or been denied it.” One may feel reasonably sure that it is this kind of solicitude, rather than any possible sanction from the crowd, which would be thought of by the author of this book as “the exact high prize through desire of which we write.”

===Chinese===
我再说一遍：这绝不是说，这类写作的报偿有多大部分能来自外界。按照罗伯特·埃瑟里奇·汤森的说法：「人写出可观的散文，全然不是为了让人读它，而是出于一个更说得通的理由——他喜欢独自玩单人纸牌。」——这话，很有些卡贝尔的味道。而即便是来自外界的报偿，其中真正不可或缺的那一部分是否从来出自大众，也大可存疑。有一位女士——她那些较为坦率的见解，如今赏给本文作者的次数已胜过从前——曾说过：「每回我听说有人想要这几本书中的某一册却无从到手，或是到手之后，却只肯赏给它一个无知之徒的轻蔑，我就觉得自己必须即刻取出那一册来，一遍又一遍地通读下去，直到我为每一个拒斥过它、或求而不得它的人，都各读过一遍为止。」人们大可以有相当把握地相信：在本书作者的心目中，会被认作「我们因渴慕之而写作的那份确切的高贵奖赏」的，正是这一种牵念，而绝非来自人群的任何可能的认可。

===Original===
Wilson Follett

Cheshire, Connecticut

May, 1920

===Chinese===
威尔逊·福莱特

康涅狄格州柴郡（Cheshire, Connecticut）

1920 年 5 月

## The Prologue / 序幕

Which Deals with the Essentials / 略论几桩根本之事

## I / 一

Writing / 写作

===Original===
It appeared to me that my circumstances clamored for betterment, because never in my life have I been able to endure the contact of unhappiness. And my mother was always crying now, over (though I did not know it) the luckiest chance which had ever befallen her; and that made me cry too, without understanding exactly why.

So the child, that then was I, procured a pencil and a bit of wrapping-paper, and began to write laboriously:

===Chinese===
在我看来，我的境况亟须改善，因为我生平从来不曾忍受得了同不幸打照面。母亲这时总是哭个不住，为的是她此生撞上的最走运的一桩机缘（这一点我当时并不知道）；她一哭，我便也哭，却说不清究竟为什么。

于是那孩子——也就是当时的我——寻来一支铅笔、一小张包装纸，开始费力地写：

===Original===
“Dear Lord

“You know that Papa died and please comfort Mama and give Father a crown of Glory Ammen

“Your lamb and very sincerely yours

“Robert Etheridge Townsend.”

===Chinese===
「亲爱的主：

「你知道爸爸死了，求你安慰妈妈，也求你给父亲一顶荣耀的冠冕，阿门

「您的小羊羔，也是您最诚挚的

「罗伯特·埃瑟里奇·汤森（Robert Etheridge Townsend）。」

===Original===
This appeared to the point as I reread it, and of course God would understand that children were not expected to write quite as straight across the paper as grown people. The one problem was how to deliver this, my first letter, most expeditiously, because when your mother cried you always cried too, and couldn’t stop, not even when you wanted to, not even when she promised you five cents, and it all made you horribly uncomfortable.

===Chinese===
重读一遍，我觉得它正中肯綮；当然，上帝也会体谅，小孩子写字原不比大人，谁也不指望他们的字行在纸上走得笔直。唯一的问题是：这头一封信，怎样投递才最快？因为母亲一哭，你总归跟着哭，停也停不住——哪怕你自己想停，哪怕她许你五分钱，也一样停不住；那滋味，叫你难受得没个底。

===Original===
I knew that the big Bible on the parlor table was God’s book. Probably God read it very often, since anybody would be proud of having written a book as big as that and would want to look at it every day. So I tiptoed into the darkened parlor. I use the word advisedly, for there was not at this period any drawing-room in Lichfield, and besides, a drawing-room is an entirely different matter.

Everywhere the room was cool, and, since the shades were down, the outlines of the room’s contents were uncomfortably dubious; for just where the table stood had been, five days ago, a big and oddly-shaped black box with beautiful silver handles; and Uncle George had lifted me so that I could see through the pane of glass, which was a part of this funny box, while an infinity of decorous people rustled and whispered. …

===Chinese===
我知道，客厅桌上那本大部头《圣经》是上帝的书。上帝多半时常翻看它：一个人写出了那么大的一部书，谁都要得意，谁都愿意天天瞧上一眼。于是我踮起脚尖，溜进了遮暗的客厅。「客厅」这个词，我是斟酌过才用的——那个年头，利奇菲尔德（Lichfield）还没有谁家里有「会客厅」呢；况且，会客厅同客厅也完全是两码事。

满屋凉浸浸的；遮帘既然全放了下来，屋里各物的轮廓便暧昧得叫人不自在。因为就在那张桌子摆着的地方，五天前停过一只又大又怪的黑箱子，配着漂亮的银把手；乔治叔叔把我抱起来，好让我透过那块玻璃——它是这只古怪箱子的一部分——朝里看，四下里则有数不清的体面人窸窸窣窣、窃窃私语。……

===Original===
I remember knowing they were “company” and thinking they coughed and sniffed because they were sorry that my father was dead. In the light of knowledge latterly acquired, I attribute these actions to the then prevalent weather, for even now I recall how stiflingly the room smelt of flowers—particularly of magnolia blossoms—and of rubber and of wet umbrellas. For my own part, I was not at all sorry, though of course I pretended to be, since I had always known that as a rule my father whipped me because he had just quarreled with my mother, and that he then enjoyed whipping me.

I desired, in fine, that he should stay dead and possess his crown of glory in Heaven, which was reassuringly remote, and that my mother should stop crying. So I slipped my note into the Apocrypha. …

===Chinese===
我记得我当时知道他们是「客人」，还当他们咳嗽、抽鼻子，是为父亲的死伤心。拿我后来获得的知识看，这些举动该归咎于当时流行的天气；因为直到如今我还记得，那屋里的气味闷得人透不过气来——花香，尤其是木兰花香——还有橡胶和湿雨伞的气味。至于我自己，我一点也不伤心——当然，伤心还是要装一装的：因为我一向心里有数，父亲照例是刚同母亲吵过嘴才拿鞭子抽我，而且抽的时候，他是快活的。

归根结底，我只巴望两件事：他从此长眠，在那遥远得叫人安心的天堂里戴他的荣耀冠冕；母亲从此别再哭。于是我把字条塞进了《次经》。……

===Original===
I felt that somewhere in the room was God and that God was watching me, but I was not afraid. Yet I entertained, in common with most children, a nebulous distrust of this mysterious Person, a distrust of which I was particularly conscious on winter nights when the gas had been turned down to a blue fleck, and the shadow of the mantelpiece flickered and plunged on the ceiling, and the clock ticked louder and louder, in prediction (I suspected) of some terrible event very close at hand.

Then you remembered such unpleasant matters as Elisha and his bears, and those poor Egyptian children who had never even spoken to Moses, and that uncomfortably abstemious lady, in the fat blue-covered *Arabian Nights*, who ate nothing but rice, grain by grain—in the daytime. … And you called Mammy, and said you were very thirsty and wanted a glass of water, please.

===Chinese===
我觉出上帝就在这屋子的什么地方，正看着我；可我并不害怕。不过，同大多数孩子一样，我对这位神秘人物也怀着一团说不清道不明的疑忌。这疑忌，一到冬夜便格外分明：煤气灯拧得只剩一粒蓝火苗，壁炉架的影子在天花板上明明灭灭、忽起忽落，挂钟嘀嗒得越来越响，预告着（我疑心）某桩近在眉睫的可怕事体。

这时你便想起种种不痛快的事：以利沙（Elisha）和他的那些熊；那些可怜的埃及孩子——他们连一句话也不曾同摩西（Moses）讲过；还有那位节食节得叫人不自在的太太，在那部蓝封皮、厚墩墩的《一千零一夜》（*Arabian Nights*）里，一粒一粒地光吃米饭——还只限白天。……于是你喊奶妈，说你口渴得很，劳驾想喝杯水。

===Original===
Today, though, while acutely conscious of that awful inspection, and painstakingly careful not to look behind me, I was not, after all, precisely afraid. If God were a bit like other people I knew He would say, “What an odd child!” and I liked to have people say that. Still, there was sunlight in the hall, and lots of sunlight, not just long and dusty shreds of sunlight, and I felt more comfortable when I was back in the hall.

===Chinese===
不过这一天，尽管我清清楚楚觉出那场可怕的检视，也千小心万小心、决不肯回头张望，到底并没有真的害怕。倘若上帝有几分像我认识的其他人，他准会说：「这孩子多古怪呀！」而我顶爱听人说这话。话虽如此，门厅里有太阳光，大片大片的太阳光——不是那种拖得老长、蒙着灰尘的一绺一绺——回到门厅里，我便自在多了。

## II / 二

Reading / 阅读

===Original===
I lay flat upon my stomach, having found that posture most conformable to the practice of reading, and I considered the cover of this slim, green book; the name of John Charteris, stamped thereon in fat-bellied letters of gold, meant less to me than it was destined to signify thereafter.

A deal of puzzling matter I found in this book, but in my memory, always, one fantastic passage clung as a burr to sheep’s wool. That fable, too, meant less to me than it was destined to signify thereafter, when the author of it was used to declare that he had, unwittingly, written it about me. Then I read again this

===Chinese===
我趴得平平的——我发觉这个姿势最宜于读书——端详着这本薄薄的绿皮书的封面。约翰·查特里斯（John Charteris）的名字，用一粒粒大腹便便的烫金字母压印在上面；此刻这个名字于我的分量，还远不及它日后注定要有的那一层意思。

这本书里，叫我迷惑的地方着实不少；但有一段奇文，始终像芒刺粘着羊毛似的粘在我的记忆里。这则寓言，此刻于我的分量，也远不及它日后注定要有的那一层意思——日后，写它的人惯于宣称，他是不知不觉之间，把我写了进去。于是我又读了一遍这则

===Original===
Fable of the Foolish Prince

===Chinese===
「愚蠢的王子」的寓言

===Original===
“As to all earlier happenings I choose in this place to be silent. Anterior adventures he had known of the right princely sort. But concerning his traffic with Schamir, the chief talisman, and how through its aid he won to the Sun’s Sister for a little while; and concerning his dealings with the handsome Troll-wife (in which affair the cat he bribed with butter and the elm-tree he had decked with ribbons helped him); and with that beautiful and dire Thuringian woman whose soul was a red mouse: we have in this place naught to do. Besides, the Foolish Prince had put aside such commerce when the Fairy came to guide him; so he, at least, could not in equity have grudged the same privilege to his historian.

“Thus, the Fairy leading, the Foolish Prince went skipping along his father’s highway. But the road was bordered by so many wonders—as here a bright pebble and there an anemone, say, and, just beyond, a brook which babbled an entreaty to be tasted—that many folk had presently overtaken and had passed the loitering Foolish Prince. First came a grandee, supine in his gilded coach, with half-shut eyes, uneagerly meditant upon yesterday’s statecraft or tomorrow’s gallantry; and now three yokels, with ruddy cheeks and much dust upon their shoulders; now a haggard man in black, who constantly glanced backward; and now a corporal with an empty sleeve, who whistled as he went.

===Chinese===
「至于更早的一切遭遇，我在此处姑且缄口。他先前自也有过几桩十足王子气派的奇遇。但关于他同头号法宝沙米尔（Schamir）的交道，以及他如何借它之力、一度赢得了太阳的妹妹；关于他同那位美貌的特罗尔妻子（Troll-wife）的往来——在这桩公案里，被他用黄油收买的猫，同被他系满彩带的榆树，都出过一臂之力；还有那位灵魂是一只红老鼠的、美丽而可畏的图林根（Thuringian）女人：这些，我们在此处都无庸置喙。况且，仙女来引路之后，愚蠢的王子早把这类交际撇开了手；那么至少他自己，总不好昧着公道，眼红他的史官也享这同一份特权。

「就这样，仙女在前头引着，愚蠢的王子沿着他父王的大道蹦蹦跳跳地走去。只是这一路两旁的奇景太多了——比如说，这儿一颗亮晶晶的石子，那儿一朵银莲花，再过去一点，一条小溪咕哝咕哝地央告人去尝一口——不多时，便有许多人赶过了磨磨蹭蹭的愚蠢的王子，把他甩在了后头。先过来的是一位大贵人，仰在他那镀金的马车里，眼皮半阖，懒洋洋地思量着昨日的国务、或者明日的风流韵事；接着是三个乡巴佬，红扑扑的脸膛，肩上积着厚厚的尘土；接着是个黑衣的憔悴汉子，走几步便回头张一眼；接着是个空着一只袖管的下士，一路走，一路吹口哨。

===Original===
“A butterfly guided every man of them along the highway. ‘For the Lord of the Fields is a whimsical person,’ said the Fairy, ‘and such is his very old enactment concerning the passage even of his cowpath; but princes each in his day and in his way may trample this domain as prompt their will and skill.’

“‘That now is excellent hearing,’ said the Foolish Prince; and he strutted.

“‘Look you,’ said the Fairy, ‘a man does not often stumble and break his shins in the highway, but rather in the byway.’ …

===Chinese===
「他们每一个人，都有一只蝴蝶沿着大道给他引路。『因为田野之主是个任性的主儿，』仙女说，『连从他这条牛道经过，他都定下了这么一条年深日久的章程；至于王子们嘛，各人有各人的日子、各人有各人的路数，只要意志和本事来得快捷，尽可以在这片地界里信马由缰。』

「『这话听来甚是中听，』愚蠢的王子说；说罢便神气活现地迈开了方步。

「『你瞧，』仙女说，『人在大道上倒难得绊跤、跌断小腿；栽跟头，多半倒在岔道上。』……

===Original===
“Thus, the Fairy leading, the Foolish Prince went skipping on his allotted journey, though he paused once in a while to shake his bauble at the staring sun.

“‘The stars,’ he considered, ‘are more sympathetic.’ …

===Chinese===
「就这样，仙女引着路，愚蠢的王子在他分定的旅途上继续蹦跳前行，只是偶尔停一停脚，朝那瞪眼看的太阳挥一挥他的哗啷棒。

「『星星嘛，』他寻思，『倒更体恤人些。』……

===Original===
“And thus, the Fairy leading, they came at last to a tall hedge wherein were a hundred wickets, all being closed; and those who had passed the Foolish Prince disputed before the hedge and measured the hundred wickets with thirty-nine articles and with a variety of instruments, and each man entered at his chosen wicket, and a butterfly went before him; but no man returned into the open country.

“‘Now beyond each wicket,’ said the Fairy, ‘lies a great crucible, and by ninety and nine of these crucibles is a man consumed, or else transmuted into this animal or that animal. For such is the law in these parts and in human hearts.’

===Chinese===
「就这样，仙女引着路，他们终于来到一道高高的树篱跟前。篱上开着一百扇小门，扇扇紧闭；先前越过愚蠢王子的那些人，正在篱前争辩不休，拿三十九条信纲、又拿各式各样的器具，丈量那一百扇小门；随后各人从自己选中的那扇门里进去，一只蝴蝶飞在他前头——可再没有一个人回到外面的旷野上来。

「『喏，每扇小门背后，』仙女说，『都安着一口大熔炉；这一百口里有九十九口，人进去不是被烧化，就是被点化成这样的畜生、那样的畜生。因为这些地方的法度如此，人心的法度也如此。』

===Original===
“The Prince demanded how if one found by chance the hundredth wicket? But she shook her head and said that none of the Tylwydd Teg was permitted to enter the Disenchanted Garden. Rumor had it that within the Garden, beyond the crucibles, was a Tree, but whether the fruit of this Tree were sweet or bitter no person in the Fields could tell, nor did the Fairy pretend to know what happened in the Garden.

“‘Then why, in heaven’s name, need a man test any of these wickets?’ cried the Foolish Prince; ‘with so much to lose and, it may be, nothing to gain? For one, I shall enter none of them.’

===Chinese===
「王子追问：倘若有人碰巧寻见了那第一百扇门，又当如何？她却摇头，说蒂尔威斯·泰格（Tylwydd Teg，威尔士语『美丽的族类』，即仙女一族）里谁也不许踏进解魅花园。据传闻，花园里、熔炉再往内，长着一棵树；可这树结的果子是甜是苦，田野里谁也说不上来；那仙女呢，也不冒充自己晓得花园里的情形。

「『那么，看在老天的分上，人又何必去试这些门里的随便哪一扇呢？』愚蠢的王子嚷道，『要赔上的这么多，兴许连半分赚头也没有？要是我，一扇也不进。』

===Original===
“But once more she shook her glittering head. ‘In your House and in your Sign it was decreed. Time will be, my Prince; today the kid gambols and the ox chews his cud. Presently the butcher cries, *Time is!* Comes the hour and the power, and the cook bestirs herself and says, *Time was!* The master has his dinner, either way, all say, and every day.’

“And the Fairy vanished as she talked with him, her radiances thinning into the neutral colors of smoke, and thence dwindling a little by a little into the vaulting spiral of a windless and a burnt-out fire, until nothing remained of her save her voice; and that was like the moving of dead leaves before they fall.

===Chinese===
「可她又摇了摇那颗亮闪闪的头。『在你命定的宫位与星宿里，这是早经注定的了。时候将至，我的王子；今天，小羊羔还在撒欢儿，牛还在倒嚼。转眼屠夫一声喊：*时辰到了！*时辰与权势一到临，厨娘便张罗起来，说一声：*时辰已过！*横竖主人总有他的正餐——人人这么说，天天都如此。』

「仙女同他说话间便隐没了：她周身的光华渐渐稀薄，化入烟的中性色里，又从那里一点一点地缩小，缩成无风的、烬冷之火上空那一股盘旋直上的烟柱，直到除了一副嗓音之外，她什么也不剩了；那嗓音，像枯叶落地之前在枝头的颤动。

===Original===
“‘Truly,’ said the Foolish Prince, ‘I am compelled to consider this a vexatious business. For, look you, the butterfly I just now admire flits over this wicket, and then her twin flutters over that wicket, and between them there is absolutely no disparity in attraction. Hoo! here is a more sensible insect.’

“And he leaped and cracked his heels together and ran after a golden butterfly that drifted to the rearward Fields. There was such a host of butterflies about that presently he had lost track of his first choice, and was in boisterous pursuit of a second, and then of a third, and then of yet others; but none of them did he ever capture, the while that one by one he followed divers butterflies of varying colors, and never a golden butterfly did he find any more.

===Chinese===
「『说实在的，』愚蠢的王子说，『我不得不承认，这是桩恼人的营生。你瞧，我方才赞赏的那只蝴蝶，掠过这扇门；她的孪生姊妹呢，又在那扇门上头扑腾；两个迷人的本领，分毫不差。嚯！这儿倒有只更明白事理些的虫子。』

「说罢他纵身一跃，两脚跟咔的一碰，追着一只金蝴蝶跑去了——那蝴蝶正往后方的田野里飘。四下里的蝴蝶多得成群结队，不多时他便追丢了自己头一个相中的，大呼小叫地追起第二只，又追第三只，又追旁的；可他一只也没逮着，只顾一只接一只地追那些五颜六色的蝴蝶；而金蝴蝶，他从此再也没能遇上过一只。

===Original===
“When it was evening, the sky drew up the twilight from the east as a blotter draws up ink, and stars were kindling everywhere like tiny signal-fires, and a light wind came out of the murky east and rustled very plaintively in places where the more ambiguous shadows were; and the Foolish Prince shivered, for the air was growing chill, and the tips of his fingers were aware of it.

“‘A crucible,’ he reflected, ‘possesses the minor virtue of continuous warmth.’

===Chinese===
「到了傍晚，天空把暮色从东方汲上来，像吸墨纸汲墨水一样；繁星到处点起，像一星一星的信号火；一阵轻风从晦暝的东边刮起，在那些影子最暧昧的所在，飒飒地响得十分哀怨。愚蠢的王子打了个寒噤：空气冷起来了，他的指尖先觉察到了。

「『熔炉嘛，』他寻思，『倒也有桩小小的德行：暖和，而且不断。』

===Original===
“And before the hedge he found a Rational Person, led hither by a Clothes’ Moth, working out the problem of the hundred wickets in consonance with the most approved methods. ‘I have very nearly solved it,’ the Rational Person said, in genteel triumph, ‘but this evening grows too dark for any further ciphering, and again I must wait until tomorrow. I regret, sir, that you have elected to waste the day, in pursuit of various meretricious Lepidoptera.’

“‘A happy day, my brother, is never wasted.’

===Chinese===
「在篱前，他遇见一位理性人，由一只衣蛾领到此地，正按照最获公认的方法，演算那一百扇小门的难题。『我差不多把它解出来了，』理性人说，带着一种斯斯文文的得意，『只是今晚天色黑得算不下去，我又只好等到明天。我很遗憾，先生，您竟甘愿糟蹋掉这一天，去追逐种种俗艳的鳞翅目。』

「『快活的一天，我的兄弟，是决计不白过的。』

===Original===
“‘That appears to me to be nonsense,’ said the Rational Person; and he put up his portfolio, preparatory to spending another night under his umbrella in the Fields.

“‘Indeed, my brother?’ laughed the Foolish Prince. ‘Then, farewell, for I am assured that yonder, as here, our father makes the laws, and that to dispute his appreciation of the enticing qualities of butterflies were an impertinence.’

===Chinese===
「『依我看，这净是胡话，』理性人说。他收拾起文件夹，预备在田野里他的伞底下再过一夜。

「『当真么，我的兄弟？』愚蠢的王子笑道。『那么，再会吧。因为我确信，那边同这边一样，立法的都是我们的父亲；而他老人家对蝴蝶种种迷人处的鉴赏，若是有人要来非议，未免太不知趣了。』

===Original===
“Thereafter, pushing open the wicket nearest to his hand, the Foolish Prince tucked his bauble under his left arm and skipped into the Disenchanted Garden; and as he went he sang, not noting that, from somewhere in the thickening shadows, had arisen a golden butterfly which went before him through the wicket.

===Chinese===
「随后，愚蠢的王子推开手边最近的一扇小门，把哗啷棒往左腋下一夹，蹦跳着进了解魅花园。他一路走去，一路唱着歌，全没留意那浓起来的暗影里、不知从哪儿飞起了一只金蝴蝶，正在他头里穿门而入。

===Original===
“Sang the Foolish Prince:

“‘Farewell to Fields and Butterflies

And levities of Yester-year!

For we espy, and hold more dear,

The Wicket of our Destinies.

“‘Whereby we enter, once for all,

A Garden which such fruit doth yield

As, tasted once, no more Afield

We fare where Youth holds carnival.

“‘Farewell, fair Fields, none found amiss

When laughter was a frequent noise

And golden-hearted girls and boys

Appraised the mouth they meant to kiss.

“‘Farewell, farewell! but for a space

We, being young, Afield might stray,

That in our Garden nod and say,

*Afield is no unpleasant place*.’”

===Chinese===
「愚蠢的王子唱道：

「『别了，田野；别了，蝴蝶，

别了，旧年的万般轻浮！

因为我们望见了，并且更珍重，

那扇命运的小门。

「『打这扇门进去，一去不返；

踏进的园子结着那样的果子：

一经尝过，便再不去旷野，

只往青春设着狂欢的地方去。

「『别了，秀美的田野：想当年，

笑声是顶寻常的声响，

金子心的姑娘和小伙，

把想亲的嘴细细估量。

「『别了，别了！只暂别一时：

趁年轻，还可去旷野游荡，

好在园中颔首笑语：

*旷野原不是个讨厌的地方*。』」

## III / 三

Arithmetic / 算术

===Original===
In such disconnected fashion, as hereafter, I record the moments of my life which I most vividly remember. For it is possible only in the last paragraphs of a book, and for a book’s people only, to look back upon an ordered and proportionate progression to what one has become; in life the thing arrives with scantier dignity; and one appears, in retrospection, less to have marched toward any goal than always to have jumped and scrambled from one stepping-stone to another because, however momentarily, “just this or that poor impulse seemed the sole work of a lifetime.”

===Chinese===
往后我也正是用这般东一笔、西一笔的方式，来记下我平生记得最鲜活的那些时刻。因为，只有在一本书的末几段里，而且只对书里的人物，才谈得上回望一条井然有序、比例匀称的来路，直通到他如今这副模样；在人生里，这东西临场时要少体面得多；回头一看，人不像朝哪个目标进军过，倒像永远在一块垫脚石与另一块垫脚石之间又跳又爬，只因为——不管多么短暂——「正是这桩或那桩可怜的冲动，俨然便成了一生唯一的事业。」

===Original===
Well! at least I have known these moments and the rapture of their dominance; and I am not lightly to be stripped of recollection of them, nor of the attendant thrill either, by any cheerless hour wherein, as sometimes happens, my personal achievements confront me like a pile of flimsy jackstraws.

===Chinese===
嗐！至少我经历过这些时刻，领教过它们君临一切的那阵狂喜。任凭哪个败兴的时辰——这种时辰有时是会来的——把我的种种成就，像一小堆轻飘飘的挑棒签子似的，撂到我面前，也休想轻易夺走我对它们的记忆，连带那一阵心悸，也一并夺不走。

===Original===
What does it all amount to?—I do not know. There may be some sort of supernal bookkeeping, somewhere, but very certainly it is not conformable to any human mathematics.

===Chinese===
这一切加起来算个什么？——我不知道。天上某处，兴许有一套超凡的簿记；只是它必定合不上人间的任何一种算术。
